@@130959341 <h> Developing nations at risk as climate adaptation finance lags <p> Investment : With financing from the Green Climate Fund and co-financing from the Government of Tuvalu , the 7-year Tuvalu Coastal Adaptation Project ( 2017-2024 ) is contributing to strengthening the resilience of one of the world 's most vulnerable countries to climate change and sea-level rise . Photo : TCAP/UNDP <p> As global temperatures rise and climate impacts intensify , developing countries are being left dangerously exposed , with finance to build climate resilience falling hundreds of billions short of what is needed to protect lives , livelihoods and economies . <p> This stark warning comes from the UN Environment Programme 's Adaptation Gap report 2025 : Running on Empty , released ahead of the COP30 climate summit in Bel ? m , Brazil , next month . It paints a sobering picture : while most nations have developed adaptation plans , the money to implement them is still nowhere near sufficient . <p> Unep estimates that by 2035 , developing countries will require between $310 billion and $365 billion annually to cope with climate impacts . Yet @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ $26 billion -- less than one-twelfth of what 's needed . This leaves a " yawning adaptation finance gap " of between $284 billion and $339 billion a year . <p> Although adaptation funding from multilateral climate funds grew in 2024 , reaching nearly $920 million ( an 86% increase on the previous five-year average ) , Unep cautions that this may prove a temporary spike , not a sustained trend . <p> " A global effort is needed to implement ambitious climate action in view of accelerating climate impacts , " the report said , noting that action on adaptation is still inadequate . <p> " Although there is clear evidence of accelerating climate impacts , changing geopolitical priorities and increasing fiscal constraints are making it more challenging to mobilise the resources needed for climate mitigation -- preventing or reducing the emission of greenhouse gases , adaptation and loss and damage . " <p> The lack of resources , action , and global attention will result in higher long-term global temperatures and associated climate impacts and risks . Yet investments in climate action far outweigh the costs of inaction @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for themselves : every $1 spent on coastal protection , for example , can prevent $14 in damages . Urban nature-based solutions can lower temperatures by more than 1 ? C , while health-focused adaptation measures reduce heat stress and save lives . <h> Missed goals , rising risks <p> If these trends persist , the report finds that the Glasgow Climate Pact target -- doubling adaptation finance from 2019 levels by 2025 to about $40 billion -- will not be met . <p> At the same time , the New Collective Quantified Goal adopted at the climate negotiations in Baku last year , which calls for $300 billion a year by 2035 for climate action in developing countries , is also deemed insufficient . <p> That financing target includes both mitigation and adaptation and is not adjusted for inflation -- meaning that real adaptation needs could reach $440 billion to $520 billion a year by 2035 . <p> In its report , Unep welcomes the Baku to Bel ? m Roadmap to US$1.3 trillion , established to scale up climate finance for low-carbon , climate-resilient development . <p> But @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ concessional , non-debt-creating instruments -- not through loans that worsen the debt burden of vulnerable nations . <p> " The Baku to Bel ? m Roadmap to raise US$1.3 trillion in climate finance could make a huge difference , but care must be taken not to increase the vulnerabilities of developing nations . " <p> Care , too , must be taken not to increase the proportion of non-concessionary debt instruments , the report outlined , warning that 58% of adaptation finance in 2022 and 2023 took the form of debt , with non-concessional loans now outpacing concessional ones . <h> Uneven progress in planning and implementation <p> There is , however , progress in planning , which is critical to effectively minimise and address current and future climate risks . Of 197 countries , 172 now have at least one national adaptation policy , strategy , or plan . However , 36 have not been updated in more than a decade , which is a risk factor for maladaptation ( failed adaptation ) . Of the 25 countries still lacking a plan , 21 are developing one . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ further , integrating adaptation across national development strategies . Nearly 60% of countries also report embedding adaptation into non-climate policies , according to biennial transparency reports ( BTRs ) submitted under the Paris Agreement to outline their progress in meeting climate pledges . <p> Unep 's analysis of more than 1 600 adaptation actions reported in the BTRs shows that most focus on biodiversity , agriculture , water and infrastructure . <p> Yet few countries report on outcomes -- such as uptake of adaptation-relevant technologies and practices , improved access to basic services , or reforestation -- and impacts that describe greater resilience , including changes in biodiversity from restoration and ecosystem protection , sufficient water availability , or improved agricultural production . <h> SA hailed for climate reporting <p> South Africa 's BTR offers a clear example of what strong climate reporting can look like . It links observed and projected threats -- from droughts and heatwaves to shifting rainfall patterns -- with the country 's most vulnerable sectors , including water and agriculture . <p> The report then shows how adaptation measures are addressing these risks . Investments @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ go hand in hand with educational campaigns . In contrast , outcomes like improved water security , healthier soils , and more resilient farmers illustrate the tangible results of these efforts . <p> Unep highlights South Africa 's approach as a model because it demonstrates consistency : hazards , impacts , adaptation actions and results are clearly connected . <p> Notably , the report shows that high-quality reporting does not require lengthy chapters or a flood of data . Even shorter sections can score highly if the narrative clearly traces how climate risks are being addressed . <p> Globally , consistency remains a challenge . On average , BTRs score just 0.44 out of 1 , with many countries struggling to link climate risks , priorities , and outcomes . <p> South Africa , along with a few others such as Egypt and Gabon , stands out for demonstrating how clear , structured reporting can strengthen both transparency and adaptation planning . <p> While public finance remains the backbone of adaptation funding , Unep stresses that private investment must increase substantially . Private sector adaptation flows are estimated at $5 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ with targeted policy support and " blended finance " mechanisms to reduce investor risk , this could rise to about $50 billion annually , meeting up to 20% of the adaptation needs for developing countries . But even that would leave a vast gap that only public finance can bridge . <p> There is a need to distinguish between financing ( who provides the money ) and funding ( who ultimately pays ) . Unep found that many so-called " innovative " financing models still transfer costs back to developing countries or households , easing donor budgets without reducing their financial burden . Fairness and transparency must be central to any future finance architecture , it noted . 
@@130996504 <h> Scientists probe Algoa Bay 's seabed for climate clues <p> An international team of scientists spent a week in Algoa Bay collecting samples from the seabed as part of a ground-breaking new project to help put the skids on climate change . <p> The premise of the fascinating new Convex Seascape Survey is that global warming is driving instability , and our terrestrial world is becoming increasingly fragile , with wildfires releasing dangerously high volumes of carbon into the atmosphere . <p> As this happens , the relative stability of the ocean 's carbon cycle is becoming increasingly important . <p> The floor of the sea anchors this stability by locking in organic carbon in the form of plankton carcasses and sea life faeces . <p> Instead of releasing carbon into the atmosphere , the organic debris drifts down into the depths in a kind of " marine snow " and accumulates on the seabed . <p> The remains of larger marine animals also contribute to this ancient injection of organic carbon . <p> Driven by carbon emissions , climate change would be much worse if the seabed was @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , according to the scientists , the factors that are damaging it need to be identified and stopped . <p> The spots where the best carbon anchoring is happening also need to be protected to boost this process , and hopefully deal climate change a blow . <p> The Convex survey is a groundbreaking five-year research initiative focused on this twin goal . <p> It is spearheaded by three UK-based entities , the Blue Marine Foundation , Exeter University and Convex Insurance , which partner with different local organisations at each site they visit . <p> The team has already explored sites off Scotland and New Zealand and has now come to Algoa Bay and linked up with Nelson Mandela University . <p> Speaking to The Herald on the research boat last week , Exeter University 's Dr Ben Harris said Algoa Bay was the perfect site for the project . <p> This was because it included an intricate mix of rich marine life and a range of geological factors and human activity including three inflowing rivers , two harbours , trawling , dredging and pollution issues . <p> The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ factors were affecting the sea floor , either improving its carbon absorption or " sequestration " capacity , or diminishing it by disturbing the sediment and releasing the remineralised carbon up through the water into the atmosphere . <p> Harris said marine zones with strong carbon sequestration capacity could provide a powerful new reason for properly policing marine protected areas , which was all too often not being done , and for creating more MPAs . <p> " Good carbon sequestration capability as a reason to protect a marine zone provides a possibly more compelling incentive than protecting an obscure species or even countering over-fishing because the role players co-ordinating the move could earn carbon credits . " <p> The carbon credit system allows companies and countries to offset their own carbon emissions by funding projects that reduce or remove emissions elsewhere . <p> Gail Fordham of the Blue Foundation said they had used a vibrocore to take the first samples in Algoa Bay . <p> Once lowered to the sea floor , the machine used vibration to drive down a large tube , capturing a metre-deep sausage of sediment @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ lab at NMU , this sausage will be sliced into patties , with each patty revealing a different moment in time , which will be pinpointed using carbon dating . <p> " We will then match that against its carbon content , and what we know was happening at that time , in terms of particular human or environmental activity . " <p> She said different creatures living on the seabed could affect the carbon cycle in their area , depending on their size and behaviour . <p> " It seems crazy to think these little invertebrates could do that but , when you consider how vast the sea floor is and how many of these creatures there are , it makes sense . <p> " We think burrowing animals could be benefiting the system by helping to bury marine snow . <p> " But their burrowing might also be disturbing carbon . It 's one of the things we hope to find out . " <p> Besides the CTD ( measuring salinity , temperature and depth ) , the main machine deployed on Wednesday was the ingenious " grab @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Institute of Aquatic Biology manning the winch , it was lowered to the sea floor for six grabs at each site , flipping open , scooping up 6-7l of sediment and snapping closed to be winched up and swung onto deck . <p> Assisted at various times through the day by different members of the team on each grab , the University of Exeter 's Dr Sabrina Sykes removed whatever little feathery , spiny and squelchy critters there were on top for separate attention , then scooped up and labelled two bottles of sediment . <p> She showed how the bottles were carefully labelled , noting all the particulars of the site . <p> " It 's important to keep track or else it all mushes into one . " <p> NMU 's Dr Frederick Mokumo spent most of the trip poring over sieves full of starfish , sponges , brittle stars , feather stars and jointed worms in a confusing jumble of sand and other debris . <p> He used a pair of tweezers to extract each of the little animals , dividing them according to their general behaviour @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Sometimes these worms look inanimate because they have built a communal cone from sand and other debris to protect themselves . <p> " In that case , I extract the whole lot and we do the further sorting in the lab , but we try do as much sorting as possible on the boat . <p> " Most important will be to link species to site and to understand how they function and how that is affecting the carbon content of that part of the seabed . <p> " But at the end we will also check the carbon content of each animal itself by subtracting its ashed weight from its actual weight . " <p> In terms of NMU 's two-step ethical invertebrate collection policy , each creature was placed into a weak solution of ethanol to stun it , before being euthanised and preserved in a strong solution of ethanol . <p> Harris said one study had found that the volume of carbon released into the atmosphere because of dredging was greater than the volume from the aviation industry . <p> " There has been some pushback against @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is why we are considering it here . " <p> He said sewage pollution , a common feature in Algoa Bay as a result of the Nelson Mandela Bay municipality 's shaky wastewater management system , was another negative . <p> " It might even push up the organic carbon for a while because it 's introducing extra nutrients -- but in the long run it will kill marine life and without that life there can be no organic carbon . <p> " Further , even as we zero in on climate change and the vital issue of reducing atmospheric carbon , we have to remember that biodiversity is also fundamental , and if we lose that , we lose everything . " <p> Artist Naomi Hart spent her time on the trip doing lightning-fast sketches of the team members as they went about their different tasks , and then some close-ups of the machines and the creatures brought to the surface . <p> She said she hoped to capture the reality of field science . <p> " I hope , by seeing it through the lens , people who @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ inspired . @@130999894 <h> Zambia 's largest bank to issue nation 's first sustainability bond <p> Zambia National Commercial Bank , or Zanaco , plans to raise $100m through the country 's first sustainability bond , its chief executive officer Mukwandi Chibesakunda said at a press briefing in Lusaka on Friday , 31 October 2025 . <p> In the last few years such bonds , issued to raise capital for projects that deliver environmental and/or social benefits , have been issued by a wide range of countries across both developed and emerging markets . <p> Zanaco 's bond will be issued in two tranches , starting with a $50m private placement within six months , followed by a $50m public placement after the initial sale . <p> Chibesakunda said the bank is in talks with private investors and will disclose their identities once agreements are finalised . <p> Funds raised via the bond will support projects in renewable energy , sustainable agriculture and climate-resilient infrastructure , in line with Zambia 's Green Growth Strategy ( 2024 -- 2030 ) and its Climate Change Act . <p> Chibesakunda described the issuance as " a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and uplift communities , " calling it a milestone for Zambia 's capital markets . <p> Africa accounts for less than 1% of the global $2.2tn green bond market , according to the Africa Policy Research Institute , highlighting a significant gap in funding for climate-related initiatives on the continent . <p> Estimates from the World Bank suggest Africa would need $2.8tn by 2030 to implement its Nationally Determined Contributions , set as part of the 2015 Paris Agreement and aimed at cutting greenhouse gas emissions and adapting to climate change . <p> That target is unlikely to be met through current climate-related budgets and donor pledges , however , based on historical trends .

We 're sitting on the stairs and praying while trying to gauge the typhoon 's strength , " the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ wind is whistling and there are sounds of things falling . The electricity went out about an hour ago , and we can not see anything . " <p> Roel Montesa , a disaster official on Leyte Island , said earlier that evacuations were " ongoing in Palo and Tanauan " , naming two of the towns hardest hit by storm surges in 2013 , when Super Typhoon Haiyan killed more than 6,000 people there . <p> Thousands of residents have also been evacuated since Sunday on neighbouring Samar island , where three-metre ( 10-foot ) surges are predicted , according to civil defence official Randy Nicart . <p> " Some local governments are resorting to forced evacuations , " he said . <p> The Philippines is hit by an average of 20 storms and typhoons each year , routinely striking disaster-prone areas where millions live in poverty . <p> With Kalmaegi , the archipelago country has already reached that average , state weather service specialist Charmaine Varilla told AFP , adding that at least " three to five more " storms could be expected by December 's end . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , governor Nilo Demerey said 10,000 to 15,000 people had been pre-emptively moved to safer areas . <p> Disaster official Joy Conales said residents of Dinagat 's Loreto town were told to evacuate to higher ground . <p> The town has a one-storey-tall dike intended to protect its centre from big waves . <p> Scientists warn that storms are becoming more powerful due to human-driven climate change .

@@131038999 <h> Cover Story , Environment <p> It 's in your food , your tea , your blood -- and possibly even your liver and brain . It 's inside us , and around us , and it 's alarming . Like the rest of the world , Africa too is grappling with the rising threat of microplastics . This persistent , nearly indestructible material could bring environmental ruin , or create financial and economic opportunity . How is the continent dealing with it ? <p> In South Africa , another load of waste enters Cape Town 's Coastal Park Landfill on a wintry Tuesday morning in September , prompting a flock of seagulls to continue its daily ritual of pecking through what seems to be a contaminated pile of plastic , food , clothes , and everything in between . <p> Two driving lanes separate the landfill from the Indian Ocean , but these opportunistic gulls have found a feeding loophole , an endless supply of human excess . On a bad day , it can be seen as a drive past mountains of stinking waste , dramatized by the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ day , the mountain is more reminiscent of a hill , but the high-pitched sounds remain . <p> Advertisement <p> Like these birds , millions across the continent depend on waste for food and income , regardless of its toxicity , which , for the majority of Africa , is spiraling out of control , like wildfire . <p> Communities from around the continent are grappling with plastic pollution , along with diminishing biomes and desolated food supplies . It 's an omnipresent reality garnering worldwide attention . In addition , scientists are fearful of another escalating threat : microplastics ( MPs ) . <p> Loading ... <p> Developed around the 1950s , plastic -- a cheap polymer produced from petrochemicals -- has become unimaginable in the modern world . It helps to transport and preserve fresh fruit and vegetables , aids in the production of smartphones and medical supplies , and is used extensively in construction and automotive components . <p> However , many plastic products are single-use -- some last for years -- and they all eventually deteriorate , their synthetic framework unable to biodegrade . Instead , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , sometimes , millions of particles , subsequently entering our environments and food chains . <p> Advertisement <p> Part Of Everyday Life ... <p> Defined as plastic particles smaller than 5mm , MPs are split into two categories : primary and secondary . The former relates to purposefully small-produced plastics , such as microbeads found in skin cleaners and toothpaste , pellets in plastic bottles and shopping bags , and microfibers in clothes and home textiles , while secondary MPs are released from the breakdown and mismanagement of primaries . <p> Cleaning a single cloth can release thousands of microfibers into wastewater , a McIlwraith et al . report shows ; while another study -- Sudheshna et al . -- identified that one wash load can shed several million microfibers . <p> We wear it , breathe it , and ingest it , without noticing . <p> Advertisement <p> Research linking the impact of MPs on human health remains inconclusive ; however , we know that plastics contain toxic chemicals , such as plasticizers , and that marine species are ingesting MPs at an unprecedented rate , many of which @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ harmful , but currently , we ca n't confirm whether particles can cross your biological barriers . Not every particle can enter your organs ; there 's filtering systems in our bodies . For us to understand its human impact , we first have to understand which particles cross at what size , " says Elvis Okoffo , a research fellow at Queensland Alliance for Environmental Health Sciences at the University of Queensland Australia , which has been studying the impact of MPs on human health since 2020 . <p> Okoffo adds : " We 're trying to measure plastics in biological tissues , using refined methods to identify single particles , because we do n't know what people are measuring . For studies identifying plastics in bodies , you need to be able to measure single particle bases . Within a few years , we should get conclusive data regarding how it impacts humans . " <p> Oceans Of Plastic <p> Advertisement <p> Ghana 's Kpeshie and Mukwei lagoons , seen as crucial to the support of aquatic biodiversity or as a habitat for several bird species , have @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ discovered 964 MP particles between these lagoons . Kpeshie , in particular , once flourished with fish , but now houses a collapsed ecosystem . <p> Unsurprisingly , commercial seafood , including mussels , oysters , and fish , as well as fruits and vegetables , have not escaped the impact of MPs -- a 2020 Yu et al . report suggested that humans may consume around 80 milligrams ( mg ) of it a day . <p> That 's the equivalent of consuming a plastic bottle cap every 12 days , and a standard plastic bag every two to three months . <p> Advertisement <p> While these MPs may be manufactured on land , their journey often becomes aquatic . They slip into drains and wastewater , wash out with storm runoff , or spill from industrial and agricultural sites -- eventually flushing into waterways . They are then ingested by marine life , and eventually end up on our plates . <p> A 2021 report by Alimi et al . revealed that 5%-100% of all aquatic organisms sampled during the study contained microplastics , including 73% of all @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of Tilapia in the Nile River ( Egypt ) ; and 98% of mussels in Cape Town ( South Africa ) . <p> The oceanic presence of this material has become so extreme that , at the going rate , there will be more synthetic particles in the oceans than fish , by weight , by 2050 , the World Economic Forum predicts . <p> It has also been revealed that MP levels may be higher in lakes near intensive agricultural activities and high populations , both of which are prevalent across Africa . This , per a Yang et al . report surveying microplastics in lake systems , which identified pellets as a primary MP found in African lakes due to the region 's widespread small-scale fishing activities , use of beads and fishing lines . <p> Advertisement <p> Bursting At The Seams <p> Africa 's population demographics are on an upward trajectory , with reports indicating that , by 2050 , it will reach nearly 2.5 billion , accounting for around 25% of the global population , a notion expected to surpass 40% before the end of the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . The Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development ( OECD ) predicts that urban areas will account for 80% of Africa 's growth , with around 159 cities exceeding one million residents by 2050 -- up from about 60 currently . <p> Exponential growth beckons for the continent , backed by housing seven of the top 10 fastest-growing economies worldwide . And while that bodes well economically , it suggests that Africa 's plastic intake , already at bursting point , will reach new heights due to ballooning markets . <p> Wastewater treatment plants , proven to remove 40% -- 99.9% of MPs ( Yang et al. , 2022 ) , are among the most effective eradicators . However , such infrastructure is lacking across Africa . <p> The continent 's inefficiency -- approximately only 10% of plastic waste generated on the continent is appropriately managed , according to the United Nations ( UN ) -- coupled with its population boom , insinuates that , unless regulations and waste-specific facilities are effectively implemented , MPs will continue rising vigorously . <p> However , Africa faces another conundrum . <p> A @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ( 5% and 4% , respectively ) , yet it contributes around 24% of the global emissions of mismanaged plastic . <p> The figure is striking , but understandable given that Africa is viewed by some as a global dumping ground . Container ships and bulk carriers transporting tons of waste reach the continent 's shores every day . <p> The lead author of Macro Problems from Microplastics , Gideon Deme , adds : " We are not even at the alarming rate yet , because it keeps increasing . We estimate that by 2040 , we will be importing a minimum of 100 million to 200 million tons of plastics in Africa , and by 2060 , we will be importing 10 billion tons of plastics . " <p> Before China banned plastic waste importation in January 2018 , it handled around 45% of the world 's plastic waste since 1992 . A 2018 study by Brooks et al. , examining the potential implications of China 's law , estimated that 111 million Mt of plastic will be displaced by 2030 . <p> " Ordinary Africans are disproportionately impacted @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ contribute the least to both . Plastic takes countless lives in Cameroon by clogging drainage systems ; in the Democratic Republic of the Congo , it 's messing with their food supply because fish are ingesting plastic pollution and dying off , " explains Angelo Louw , Greenpeace International 's content editor and documentarian , to FORBES AFRICA . <p> Economic recoveries ( post-2008 recession and pandemic ) and free trade agreements , such as the U.S.-sanctioned African Growth and Opportunity Act ( AGOA ) , further increased Africa 's plastic intake . AGOA opened the doors for synthetic-heavy manufacturing giants , such as Lesotho 's China-backed textile boom , as nations rushed to establish jobs and investment . <p> A Deme , Ewusi-Mensah et al . report identified Africa as a major global MP contributor through city dust ( 22% ) , and microbeads ( 16% ) , but ranked low in textile washing ( 4% ) , compared to China 's 28% . <p> Inhumane and inefficient landfills -- renowned MP hotspots abundant across Africa -- are another tedious battle the continent faces ; an estimated 38% of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " Landfills are very bad . In many African cities , you see huge ones , often in open areas . They overflow with single-use plastics that , under sun and rain , break into smaller pieces . But because they are n't biodegradable , decomposers ca n't act on them , and over time they shrink into MPs that enter the soil , and then , the food chain , " Deme states . <p> Lagos , in particular , reportedly generates approximately 13,000 Mt of waste daily . Once nestled on the city 's outskirts , Olasosun -- a 100-acre dump , one of Africa 's largest -- now intertwines with the northern suburbs of Ojota , Ketu , and Oregun . <p> It 's estimated that 57.4 million Mt of e-waste was produced worldwide in 2021 . Meanwhile , Ghana has become a depository , on the continent , for e-waste , the fastest-growing stream of solid waste worldwide , reports show . <p> Having said this , large swaths of Ghana 's informal economy depends on e-waste . The Institute for Security Studies revealed that it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ predicts , contributes between $105 million and $268 million to the economy , sustaining approximately 200,000 people . <p> Kenya too , like many African countries , is being inundated by fast fashion . Nairobi 's Dandora dumping site , nestled between suburbs , is a constant frenzy of people and birds foraging through waste to find value . <p> A 2023 report by the Changing Markets Foundation discovered that over 900 million items of used clothing were exported to Kenya in 2021 alone , of which around 20%-50% was deemed waste , and a significant amount likely contained plastic-based fibers that do n't biodegrade . <p> " When it comes to fast fashion , the clothes are not made with high-quality fabrics . So , when people donate clothes , it 's often spoiled before it reaches Africa . The recipient nation opens the goods and realizes they can not use much , so they just dump them , " Ewusi-Mensah comments . <p> Further down the continent , South Africa 's waste infrastructure was exposed in a 2024 audit , disclosing that 85.8% of the 189 landfills inspected @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Lining In A Stack Of Plastic <p> It 's worth noting that waste in Africa supports millions , meaning outright bans could be catastrophic . Circular solutions offer a practical way to convert waste into both value and jobs . <p> By managing Kigali 's landfills , Rwandan waste management and recycling company , Depot Kalisimbi , has recycled over three million kilograms ( kg ) of plastic waste into flexible materials , pavers , and bricks , and produced over one million kilograms of compost from organic waste streams . <p> " The challenge of MPs is urgent , but with innovation , regional cooperation , and strong policy enforcement , we can protect ecosystems and livelihoods while building a circular economy that benefits all , " Depot Kalisimbi says . <p> The Flipflopi Project , a non-profit based in Kenya , also showcases how harnessing waste can spark awareness and local empowerment . In 2019 , they built a sailing boat from recycled plastic for expeditions across East Africa 's coastline ; while on Limu island , where over 400kg of plastic has been recovered , they create @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ goal is to design a model that can be adopted across the continent , and the only way to do that is through a holistic systemic approach on the ground , " Hannah Evans , Flipflopi 's Digital Communications Manager , tells FORBES AFRICA . <p> ... Something In The Soil <p> A common misconception is that MPs drift only through water and air , but research shows they 're just as prevalent in the soil . Ragoobur et al . ( 2021 ) verified around 230 microplastics particles kg-1 in agricultural soils near a Mauritian landfill , with 96% found in the deeper soil layers . <p> " The soil and terrestrial environment are often overlooked in MPs research . In reality , soils are one of the largest sinks for MPs , through biosolids , compost , landfills , wastewater irrigation , atmospheric deposition , tire wear particles , and litter breakdown . Once in the soil , MPs can interact with soil microbes and potentially transfer into the food chain , " Okoffo adds . <p> The ability of MPs to alter soil structure could also insinuate @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ industry , the continent 's biggest employer . <p> The sector employs around 49% of sub-Saharan Africa 's workforce , World Bank data indicates . Food demand is set to soar amid Africa 's demographic boom , emphasizing the importance of food security . <p> " There 's the trophic level system . If people do n't eat much seafood , they may eat cattle and probably vegetables . Cattle ingest MPs through eating grass , so when you eat their meat , the MPs carried in their blood end up in your body , no matter how you cook it . The same applies for plant produce , " Deme clarifies . <p> Plastics ' relationship with climate change is another threat to agriculture . The production , transport , and degradation of plastic exude vast amounts of carbon dioxide and methane , greenhouse gases synonymous with driving the regularity of droughts , floods , and cyclones . <p> Extreme weather then ravages farmlands . The drought in Zimbabwe in 2024 -- reportedly the worst in four decades -- triggered a 72% drop in corn production . Meanwhile , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , and 41,562 acres of cropland , including maize , were destroyed in May 2025 following severe floods . <p> Humans are adept at handling natural particles , but their ability to handle MPs remains scientifically obscure . Organisms are typically unable to discern between food and non-food , suggesting that MPs could sync with dietary intake . <p> Toxicological studies link MPs to carrying an array of toxic chemicals , such as polyvinyl chloride , a heat stabilizer and plasticizer , and titanium oxide , a common additive found in plastics proven to provoke cytotoxicity on colon cells and epithelial tissues ; other chemicals include phthalates , flame retardants , and UV stabilizers . <p> Numerous heavy metals associated with cancers and cognitive skills deterioration are also found in MPs , including aluminum , mercury , selenium , and lead . They simultaneously enter the body and affect its wellbeing . <p> Ingestion , whether through the food chain , drinking from plastic bottles , or swallowing floating particles , is the primary source of human exposure , the rate at which this happens , Okeke et al . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on the size and volatility of the additive , pH , temperature of the surrounding medium ( air , water , or soil ) , and the polymer 's permeability . <p> MPs have been detected in nearly every part of the human body , including blood , bone marrow , placentas , organ systems , and even in breast milk . However , deciphering the exact number of MPs remains ambiguous . <p> A 2025 report on the bioaccumulation of MPs in decedent human brains revealed nearly 5,000mg of MPs per gram -- the equivalent to a standard tablespoon of sugar -- over 1,000mg more than 2016 's findings . In comparison , the same study found under 600mg in the liver and kidneys during both examinations . <p> Intentional Impact <p> Governments play a vital role in addressing plastic pollution , and while 34 African nations have banned single-use plastics , many have failed to implement this effectively . <p> " Most of the solutions in Africa are segregated . The sad part is that everyone is doing something , whether through NGOs or private companies , but @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ because government support is not there ... Rwanda is the only nation that has got it right , " Ewusi-Mensah says . <p> Having banned the use and importation of non-biodegradable packaging bags in 2009 , Rwanda may be setting the blueprint for Africa 's green economy , supported by strict governance , from import fines to raising public awareness . <p> " Why Rwanda succeeded was because it passed the responsibility to local leaders : the bottom-top approach . Most African countries do the top-bottom approach , which never works , " Deme explains . <p> Policies that foster a circular economy -- through recycling , repurposing , and recovery -- promote innovation and acceptance . Yet , in Africa , only eight countries have recycling mandates , with Morocco and Zimbabwe the only ones setting clear targets , Deme et al . confirms . <p> While effective legislation is vital , it 's imperative that it coincides with infrastructure upgrades and the establishment of cheap plastic alternatives ; otherwise , Africa , ripe with poverty , will seemingly continue using the most affordable choice , regardless of the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ obstacles , but governments should be reminded of a powerful tool at their disposal : education . Progress starts with changing perceptions . Without awareness , people struggle to compute the severity of littering or using single-use plastics . <p> " If we are not tackling people 's behavior through education , we will never tackle plastic pollution . It should be part of every school 's curriculum , " emphasizes Ewusi-Mensah . <p> Research guides informed decision-making , yet , Africa accounts for just 1.63% of global plastic research , while , according to researcher , Edmond Sanganyado , most African countries spend less than 1% of their GDP on research and development . <p> The continent 's complexities suggest , from a regulatory standpoint , a one-size-fits-all approach wo n't work . To tackle plastic pollution and , thus , MPs effectively , it will take a continental effort spurred by research , education , collaboration , infrastructure , and innovation . <p> It 's a lot to juggle , but then again , so is plastic . <p> The Plastic Busters : <p> While Africa faces many @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ array of unyielding individuals willing to help navigate the turbulence . @@131041478 <h> Global backing for GAIA climate fund reaches $600m at first close <p> The GAIA Climate Loan Fund ( GAIA ) has completed its first closing with $600m . This fund will be utilised to provide loans primarily for climate adaptation projects in areas that are most severely affected by climate change and are least equipped to respond . The fund aims to reach a total size of $1.48 billion , with a final closing expected in 2027 . <h> New approach to climate finance <p> GAIA marks a new approach to climate finance by providing long-term loans to sovereign , sub-sovereign , quasi-sovereign and state-owned entities , including municipalities , development banks and state-owned utilities , across 19 emerging and developing countries . <p> A minimum of 25% of commitments will be deployed in Least Developed Countries ( LDCs ) and Small Island Developing States ( SIDS ) , where financing needs are greatest . <p> At least 70% of capital will be dedicated to climate adaptation -- such as sustainable agriculture , water management , ecosystem resilience and climate-smart infrastructure -- with up to 30% supporting mitigation , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ deployment , GAIA aims to benefit an estimated 19 million people and create more than 11,000 permanent jobs ; avoid 30 million tonnes of CO2 equivalent emissions per year , deliver 700 MW of renewable energy capacity and 36,000 GWh of clean generation annually , and improve the climate resilience of over 5,000 km ? of natural resources . <p> GAIA uses a blended finance structure that combines public and private investment , with public funding deployed strategically to reduce risk and mobilise private capital . <p> The facility is structured with a junior tranche funded by concessional partners and a senior tranche for commercial investors , alongside a dedicated FX facility for local currency lending and a parallel Technical Assistance Facility to strengthen project preparation , ESG readiness , performance and impact outcomes . <p> FinDev Canada has also provided additional grant funding for both the FX and TA facilities , while MUFG serves as the origination partner for the fund . <h> Sustainable development <p> While climate change is a global challenge , its impacts fall most heavily on developing and emerging economies , where communities often lack @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , droughts and extreme heat . <p> Projects that build climate resilience have traditionally relied on public funding , which is constrained by limited budgets and competing priorities , and are often overlooked by commercial investors . <p> By providing private credit to public and quasi-public entities , GAIA aims to help tackle one of the biggest barriers in the adaptation space : the shortage of financing for infrastructure most exposed to climate risk . <p> This model proves that blended finance can unlock institutional capital for sectors critical to resilience , creating a pathway for sustainable development where it is needed most .

In the years following the 2008 global financial crisis , central banks were " the only game in town " to borrow the title of a prominent book on the subject . <p> Fiscal policy was in retreat due to an unnecessary ( at the time ) obsession with government debt , and central banks stepped into this vacuum . The Fed embarked on several rounds of quantitative easing , and it was the ECB that ultimately ended the eurozone debt crisis with President Mario Draghi 's " whatever it takes " commitment . <p> When the Covid crisis hit , central banks again stepped in forcefully , but this time there was fiscal stimulus too . An independent central bank can take a view beyond the next election cycle to keep inflation in check longer-term . <p> But we should also acknowledge that the role of central banks has continuously evolved over time and will continue to do so . The oldest central banks such as the Bank of England simply existed to help the government borrow money . By the early 20th century , crisis fighting became more important and the Fed was created in the wake of the Panic of 1907 . <p> The early 1950s were an era of financial repression , where central banks kept rates low to help governments reduce debt incurred to fight the war . The global wave of central bank independence around the 1980s and 1990s was largely in response to the inflation crisis of the 1970s , while the unorthodox policies of the 2010s were in response to the 2008 crisis . <p> The next crisis might well be related to government debt , which could require a different reaction function @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ about all this . @@131052400 <h> Richest 1% capture 41% of new wealth as global inequality hits crisis point <p> The new G20 report finds that 83% of countries ( representing 90% of the global population ) meet the World Bank 's definition of high inequality . ( Simphiwe Nkwali ) <p> Between 2000 and 2024 , the world 's richest 1% captured 41% of all new wealth , while the bottom half of the global population gained just 1% , according to a new G20 report released ahead of the G20 leaders ' summit later this month . <p> Commissioned by President Cyril Ramaphosa and co-ordinated by the extraordinary committee of independent experts on global inequality , the report highlights the need for " rewriting tax rules to ensure fair taxation of multinationals and the ultrawealthy " . <p> The report finds 83% of countries ( representing 90% of the global population ) meet the World Bank 's definition of high inequality . These countries are seven times more likely to experience democratic decline than more equal societies . <p> Drawing on data from the World Inequality Lab , an analysis shows that the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 2000 , while the poorest 50% gained just $585 over the same period , adjusted for inflation . Though income gaps between individuals have narrowed due largely to growth in China , the wealth divide between the Global North and South remains substantial . <p> The study calls for the establishment of an independent international panel -- modelled on the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change ( IPCC ) -- to monitor inequality trends , assess their causes and consequences , and evaluate policy responses for governments and global institutions . <p> The report warns that successive crises , including the Covid-19 pandemic , the war in Ukraine and rising trade tensions , have created a " perfect storm " that is intensifying poverty and inequality . One in four people globally now regularly skip meals , even as billionaire wealth reaches record highs . <p> " The world understands that we have a climate emergency ; it 's time we recognise that we face an inequality emergency too , " said Nobel Prize-winning economist Joseph Stiglitz , who chaired the six-member expert committee behind the report . <p> " It @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's a problem for our economy and our politics too . Our committee felt strongly that some of the worst effects of inequality are on democracy , " he said , adding that inequality was a " crisis in need of concerted action " . <p> " The necessary step to taking this action is for policymakers , political leaders , the private sector , journalists and academia to have accurate and timely information and analysis of the inequality crisis . " <p> Imraan Valodia , professor in economics , pro vice-chancellor for climate , sustainability &amp; inequality at Wits University and one of the six independent experts , said , " Every country has scope to address inequality at the national level , and it is in every country 's strategic interest to co-operate internationally . " <p> The report outlines several areas for policy action by the G20 and its members , including : <p> Reforming international economic rules such as intellectual property and tax frameworks to ensure multinationals and the ultrawealthy pay fair shares . <p> National action , such as pro-worker regulation , curbing excessive corporate @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ services , and implementing more progressive tax and spending policies . <p> Exploring new models of co-operation on tax , trade and the green transition amid growing geopolitical volatility . <p> " While there is a wealth of academic literature about inequality , there is not a single central body that assesses the global state of inequality , " Valodia noted . <p> The committee submitted the report to Ramaphosa on Tuesday afternoon . <p> According to the president , the report " should be seen as a blueprint for greater equality " and as a document that places " inequality on the international agenda " . <p> " It correctly asserts that inequality is , in the end , a betrayal of people 's dignity , an impediment to inclusive growth and a threat to democracy itself . " <p> He said the report provided the government with " clear actions that we can take " , adding that the UN had also been briefed on its contents . " We will be calling for an institution to be set up , " he said . <p> " This @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and it will go down in history as one of the very important reports ... to come out of a group of experts , " Ramaphosa said .

@@131156795 <p> In the same month that the UN secretary-general confirmed the world will not meet the target of limiting global warming to below 1.5 ? C above preindustrial levels , the Nobel Prize was awarded to three economists . Their research demonstrates how technological innovation , from industrial machinery to AI , has repeatedly driven economic growth by improving productivity and expanding opportunity . <p> This recognition of innovation stands in contrast to the environmental consequences of that very progress . While technology has lifted economies , it has also contributed to rising emissions and resource depletion . <p> Faced with this tension , society often looks to history for reassurance . Past warnings of collapse have not always come true . Lord Kelvin underestimated the sun 's lifespan due to limited understanding of nuclear fusion . Thomas Malthus predicted food shortages that never materialised . <p> However , today 's climate warnings are different . The Planetary Boundaries Science Lab recently confirmed that seven of the nine boundaries essential for sustaining life have been breached , with ocean acidification added this year . These are not speculative forecasts but @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ expressions of concern remain muted . A " make everything great again " mindset has taken hold , driven by confidence in technology , especially AI , to solve or outpace global challenges . Financial markets are surging , regulators are retreating and companies are quietly abandoning climate commitments in favour of profit . <p> This shift raises a deeper question : have we returned to the days of unrestricted corporate capitalism , with the principles of King IV governance quietly set aside ? There is clearly a lot of uncertainty about the future path of the global economy . <p> The IMF has estimated that the cost of coping with climate change is far more severe than the cost of mitigating it . Given that climate change is here to stay , why have financial markets seemed to shrug it off ? <p> The most optimistic scenario is that the IMF and others are simply wrong . The AI boom could usher in a new era of innovation and productivity . In this view technology will resolve the challenges facing society , and allow progress without trade-offs . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ leaders such as Donald Trump and Xi Jinping , for policies that favour national champions to gain economic advantage . This approach contrasts with the view that the biggest contributors to climate change should bear the societal costs of their actions and that global regulations are necessary to manage global challenges . <p> A more sceptical perspective suggests that financial markets , with their characteristic short-term focus , have yet to fully reckon with the long-term consequences of global warming . Instead , their attention remains fixed on the present earnings cycle , which has exceeded expectations . <p> Recently , the Prudential Authority released its roadmap on climate risk for financial institutions . This guidance note expects financial institutions to carefully assess their resilience to climate risk . <p> At the same time , the US is clearly rolling back its climate commitments and allowing its companies to compete without considering the nonfinancial implications of their decisions . This makes it more difficult for other countries to compete with the US and follow through with their own climate commitments . <p> What does this all mean for investors and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to become despondent . However , despite sustained fossil fuel subsidies from vested interests , market incentives are leaning towards cleaner energy . <p> Despite worsening planetary boundaries , corporate earnings forecasts have risen , recession fears have eased and most central banks plan to keep lowering interest rates through 2026 . And , just as those three Nobel prize-winning economists showed , innovation often happens when one least expects it . <p> I began this article sceptical of anyone 's ability ( including my own ) to confidently forecast the future in the absence of perfect knowledge . To navigate an uncertain future , investors should remain agile , ready to seize the rewards of innovation while not ignoring the real and rising costs of climate inaction . Positioning portfolios for both possibility and risk is not just prudent ; it is essential . <p> Crosoer is Chief Investment Officer at PPS Investments . <p> Would you like to comment on this article ? @@131156971 <h> The fiscal anatomy of the next pandemic <p> Fiscal constraints : One to One mobile clinc attending to patients of Lucingweni village from the O.R . Tambo district , Eastern Cape on February 28 , 2024 . Pandemics are less about future viruses and more about present budgets . Photo : Oupa Nkosi <p> The Global Council on Inequality , Aids and Pandemics has issued a warning that turns the usual pandemic logic on its head . <p> The next global health emergency , it argues , will not be triggered by a virus but by the economic conditions that allow disease to spread unchecked . <p> Debt , austerity and collapsing public budgets are becoming the true accelerants of future pandemics , leaving countries too fiscally suffocated to respond , even if science delivers the tools to intervene . <p> The findings were released in Johannesburg this week at the Gordon Institute of Business Science ( GIBS ) , where members of the council presented a two-year , evidence-heavy investigation into how inequality fuels pandemics and how pandemics , in turn , deepen inequality . <p> The launch @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ global South , signalling a shift in terrain . <p> Pandemic preparedness is no longer being discussed solely in hospitals and laboratories . It has entered treasuries , debt offices and credit-rating meetings . <p> What gives the warning added weight is timing . South Africa will chair the G20 leaders ' summit in 2025 -- the same G20 that controls 85% of global GDP and more than 90% of lending power . <p> The council 's plea is blunt : if pandemic preparedness is not treated as a fiscal issue in the G20 agenda , the next outbreak will repeat the same cycle -- science succeeds , but politics and finance fail . <p> Nobel Prize-winning economist , former chief economist of the World Bank and one of the architects of the post-2008 global financial reform agenda , Joseph Stiglitz , co-chairs the council . He said the starting point is no longer biological risk but political and economic choice . <p> " If governments enter the next pandemic already trapped in debt and austerity , you have lost before the virus arrives , " he told the audience @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ It shapes who gets infected , who gets treated , who recovers and whose economy survives . " <p> The council argues that pandemics do not become global catastrophes because viruses mutate faster than scientists can act . They escalate because health systems collapse under the weight of fiscal constraints , because vaccine manufacturing is concentrated in a few countries , and because austerity policies hollow out states ' ability to respond . <p> Once a pandemic strikes , the poorest lose income first and recover last . That widening inequality , in turn , creates the conditions for the next outbreak . The report calls it a " self-feeding cycle of vulnerability " . <p> Winnie Byanyima , executive director of UNAids , said the evidence is now overwhelming . <p> " We are living with cutting-edge science , early-warning systems and record innovation , yet pandemics are becoming more frequent and more destructive . The reason is not a lack of technology . It is the structural inequality that decides who gets the technology and who does not . " <p> The council 's argument comes at a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ health spending has fallen in real terms for the first time in two decades . More than 50 countries are in , or near , debt distress . Over 100 low- and middle-income countries now spend more on interest repayments than on health . <p> The warning is not theoretical : the world is walking into the next pandemic with weaker buffers than it had in 2020 . <p> The timing is deliberate . As South Africa prepares to host the G20 leaders ' summit , the council is warning that the next pandemic will not be stopped by science but by balance sheets . <p> A world already trapped in debt , austerity and shrinking health budgets will not have the fiscal oxygen to respond , no matter how fast a vaccine is discovered . <p> The council wants inequality treated the same way the world now treats climate risk : measured , tracked , and backed by enforceable policy . <p> For Monica Geingos , former first lady of Namibia and council member , the lesson is simple . <p> " Breakthrough medicines exist , but they are @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ That is not a scientific failure . It is a failure of power , access and political choice . Inequality is not just unfair . It is deadly . " <p> She pointed to Namibia 's experience during Covid-19 , when the country had the money to buy vaccines but still struggled to access supplies . <p> " We were told low-income countries could not afford vaccines . But that was not the full story . Some of us could pay . We just could not get a place in the queue . " <p> The council 's report recommends automatic intellectual property waivers during pandemics , advance financing for regional vaccine production , and new incentives for innovation that reward discovery without locking it behind a monopoly . <p> He added , " When 60% of your tax revenue goes to creditors , you are not sovereign . You are drained . And a drained state can not protect its people from a pandemic . " <p> The council proposes a new pandemic financing facility , backed by IMF Special Drawing Rights , that would release funds automatically when @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ waiting for donor pledges . No austerity negotiations in exchange for loans . <p> " We already know pandemics will happen , " Stiglitz said . " What is irrational is pretending every one of them is a surprise . " <p> Byanyima echoed this , arguing that the world should have learned after HIV , Ebola and Covid-19 that health security can not be built on charity . <p> " Pandemics expose the truth about how societies value human life . You find out very quickly who is disposable . That is why we are saying inequality is not a development issue . It is a threat multiplier . " <p> The report also calls for a permanent global body to monitor inequality , modelled on the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change . <p> " What climate did right was make the crisis measurable , " Stiglitz said . " Once you quantify the problem , you force markets , governments and regulators to respond . We need the same for inequality . " <p> " A few years ago , we had 14 departments of health , each @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . That is what apartheid looked like in the health system , " he said . <p> " We abolished that in the Constitution , but we wake up today and we still have two healthcare systems . One for the rich , one for the poor . The moment you divide people , you can forget about fighting a pandemic . " <p> Equality , he said , is not ideology but operational logic . <p> " In HIV , we learned that if you test and treat everyone equally , mortality drops . Inequality is what kills . " <p> The warning lands at a moment when fiscal tightening is accelerating . Global health budgets are being cut in real terms , donor spending is flat , and at least 52 countries are in , or near , debt distress . <p> The council concludes that the next pandemic will hit a world with less financial oxygen than it had in 2020 and that this , not the biology of a new virus , is the real threat . <p> What makes the council 's warning different from @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ about future viruses and more about present budgets . Pandemics , it argues , have become financial events long before they become medical ones . <p> " The next outbreak is already underway , " the report reads . " It is taking place in debt offices , in credit-rating downgrades , in austerity programmes and in the quiet shrinking of public-health payrolls . " <p> The G20 will decide whether that continues . As host , South Africa has the rare ability to put inequality on the same diplomatic footing as climate change -- not as a moral issue but as a systemic risk to markets , stability and life expectancy . If the G20 treats pandemics solely as a medical problem , the world will repeat the pattern : science races ahead , finance lags , and the poorest pay first and longest . <p> The kicker is stark . The world now has the scientific capacity to control most pandemics . What it no longer has is the fiscal capacity . If economic conditions remain unchanged , the next virus will do precisely what Covid-19 did : @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are weakest , and turn recession into depression . <p> As Stiglitz put it , " Viruses exploit biology . Pandemics exploit inequality . And we are running out of time to decide which one we want to fight . "

@@131157000 <h> Key topics : <p> Sign up for your early morning brew of the BizNews Insider to keep you up to speed with the content that matters . The newsletter will land in your inbox at 5:30am weekdays . Register here . <p> Support South Africa 's bastion of independent journalism , offering balanced insights on investments , business , and the political economy , by joining BizNews Premium . Register here . <p> If you prefer WhatsApp for updates , sign up to the BizNews channel here . <p> By Sarah Venter* <p> Baobabs are sometimes called " upside-down trees " , because their branches look like roots reaching skywards . Of the eight species of baobab in the world , six are confined to Madagascar , one to northern Australia and one species , Adansonia digitata , is found across the savannah regions of continental Africa . <p> They 're more than striking trees ; they are cornerstones of African ecosystems and livelihoods . They provide fruit , fibre , medicine and shelter for both people and wildlife . But it 's their night-blooming flowers and partnership with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ hold the secrets of their evolution and future survival . <p> Baobabs have huge white flowers that are visited at night by bats and moths to drink their sweet-tasting nectar . While feeding on the nectar , the flower covers its nocturnal visitors with pollen which they carry to the next flower they visit . This transfers pollen from the male part ( anther ) of one flower to the female part ( stigma ) of the next flower . <p> Baobab flowerSarah Venter <p> Without this pollination , the trees would not be able to produce nutritious fruit , which African people have eaten for thousands of years , or seeds to grow the next generation of baobabs . <p> I 'm a baobab ecologist who has studied these trees for 18 years . In my latest research , my team studied 284 baobabs across west ( Ghana ) , east ( Kenya ) , and southern Africa ( South Africa , Namibia , Botswana ) to see which animals pollinated their flowers . We observed bats and moths for 205 hours , filmed and caught bats to identify @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ also compared the flowers ' shapes , nectar , and scents across the regions . <p> Cameras monitoring baobab flowersSarah Venter <p> Our study found that the baobabs have different pollinators in different regions and have each adapted their flowers to suit those pollinators . <p> The baobabs are all genetically the same species , but their floral traits , shape , scent and nectar have evolved to suit the different pollinators in each region . <p> It takes thousands of years for these changes to happen . This also means the trees are deeply dependent on their relationships with either bats or moths . If these creatures go into a decline because of climate change , the trees might not reproduce . This would endanger not just the baobab species but the web of life that depends on it . <p> More research is needed about the animals that are pollinating baobab flowers and how important they are to the survival of baobabs in future . <h> Safeguarding Africa 's tree of life <p> Protecting pollinators is more than preserving biodiversity . It safeguards the continuity of one of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ its shade . <p> For restoration and conservation , one crucial lesson stands out : when selecting baobab seeds and seedlings they must match the pollinators of each region . Flowers emit fragrance and produce volumes of nectar suitable for their pollinators . If flowers fail to attract the proper visitors , they can not produce fruit or seeds . For example , a baobab tree that is adapted to bats may not thrive where only moths remain . <p> West Africa : The main visitor to the baobab trees is the straw-coloured fruit bat ( Eidolon helvum ) , a large species that feeds while hanging upside down from branches . The baobab flowers here reflect their companion : they 're big , with long stalks and deep pools of nectar . The bats rotate the flowers with their thumbs and the pollen brushes their heads and chests . The bats carry this to other blossoms , setting in motion a new generation of trees . <p> East Africa : Here , the smaller Egyptian fruit bat ( Rousettus aegyptiacus ) dominates . These bats crash-land directly onto flowers @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ flowers have therefore evolved to be smaller and sturdier , with less nectar but still enough to encourage repeated visits through the night . <p> Southern Africa : No bats visit baobab trees in this region . Instead , a variety of moths fill the role of pollinators . Some hover delicately ; others land on the flowers themselves . The flowers here are smaller , with drooping petals and wider stigmas . These subtle adaptations nudge moths into direct contact with pollen-bearing parts . <p> Sarah Venter <h> Secrets of flower form , nectar and scent <p> We also analysed the flowers and found a range of differences that mirror the feeding styles of bats and moths : <p> Shape : In bat regions , petals fold back . This makes space for the bats to land or hang on the flowers . In moth regions , petals droop , encouraging close contact and effective pollen transfer . <p> The length of the peduncle ( the stalk that attaches the flower , and later the fruit , to the branch ) : Flowers on west African baobabs have long @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . In east Africa , shorter stalks reduce wobbling when smaller bats land directly on flowers . <p> Nectar : Large bats encouraged the development of nectar-rich flowers in west Africa . East African flowers produce less nectar for smaller bats , and southern African baobab flowers provide only drops of nectar , just enough for moths . <p> Baobab flower with long peduncle.Sarah Venter <p> Stigma position : In southern Africa , some flowers have short and wide stigmas -- the part of the female reproductive organ which receives the pollen before it is transferred to the ovary buried below the stamen ball . This is unlike the fully protruding stigmas of west and east African baobab flowers that are visited by bats . For moths , this positioning increases the chance that they brush against the flower 's reproductive parts , forcing them to pollinate the flower . <h> Our future rests on wings <p> Birds , bees and beetles do not pollinate baobab flowers , so baobabs rely on moths and bats for survival . Baobab trees can cope with a wide range of environmental and climatic @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to climate change . <p> Our research shows that even giants depend on delicate partnerships , in this case , with the smallest of night-time visitors . Protecting these pollinators means protecting the baobabs themselves , and with them , the communities and ecosystems that depend on them . <p> Ghana research teamSarah Venter <p> *Sarah Venter is a Baobab Ecologist , University of the Witwatersrand <p> *This article was originally published by The Conversation and has been republished with permission . 
@@131157305 <h> INSIGHT Research that matters must translate into interventions and policies <p> November 07 , 2025 at 02:50 pm <h> Mphumzi Mdekazi <p> Mphumzi Mdekazi ( SUPPLIED ) <p> Research is not just an academic exercise . The findings from these pivotal studies often shape the policies that govern our societies , strengthen initiatives aimed at bringing about meaningful change , drive advancements in technology and health care , and influence the education of future generations . <p> Indeed , the value of research lies both in knowledge creation and in translation to action . <p> Mphumzi Mdekazi ( SUPPLIED ) <p> In SA , this is particularly important : we live in a society marked by profound inequalities , legacies of exclusion , high burdens of disease , socioeconomic vulnerability and the imperative of development . <p> Research that does not speak to these realities ' risks being disconnected , academically elegant but socially irrelevant . <p> Researchers can play their part . However , if politicians are not willing to implement research findings particularly around issues of the socioeconomic balance , such as land rights , land restitution @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ becomes irrelevant . <p> A key shift in research thinking is the move from traditional , sometimes extractive research ( where researchers come into a community , gather data , depart , publish ) toward what is often called engaged research ( ER ) . <p> This means research is done with communities , not only on them . <p> It means agenda-setting includes community voices ; methodologies are sensitive to context ; data collection and interpretation consider local meaning ; findings are returned and operationalised for real-world change . <p> The HSRC provides meaningful examples of how this shift is taking root . <p> For instance , its Centre for Community-Based Research ( CCBR ) in Sweetwaters ( a rural valley outside Pietermaritzburg ) works directly with community stakeholders , trains residents as fieldworkers and partners with community organisations to ensure research into HIV , maternal health and other critical issues is aligned with community needs . <p> This example shows how research that matters is not an abstract -- it responds to specific populations , addresses marginalised groups and seeks to generate data that translates into interventions and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in which research that matters is most needed in SA . While many could be named , I highlight several inter-related ones : <p> a ) Health and epidemiology : Given the burden of disease and the challenges in health systems delivery , research that helps adapt , innovate and scale health solutions is vital ; <p> b ) Inequality , poverty , social justice and inclusion : SA 's history of apartheid and its ongoing legacies mean that research on inequality , social exclusion , tenure security , land rights , social mobility and rural development remains urgent ; <p> c ) Innovation , technology and the Fourth Industrial Revolution ( 4IR ) : To lift SA 's developmental trajectory , research that supports the adoption of new technologies , digitalisation , automation , data science and the integration of innovation into local industry is critical . <p> But innovation must not simply replicate global models -- it must be relevant to local contexts , appropriate to local communities and inclusive ; <p> d ) Environment , sustainability , climate change : Research that helps local communities adapt , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ through differences ( a principle of engaged research ) and that addresses spatiality ( urban/rural , global/local ) becomes central ; and <p> e ) Education , capacity building and transformation:Research that matters also addresses the pipeline : the development of researchers from previously disadvantaged groups ; the inclusion of women ; @@131170131 <p> In the same month that the UN secretary-general confirmed the world will not meet the target of limiting global warming to below 1.5 ? C above preindustrial levels , the Nobel Prize was awarded to three economists . Their research demonstrates how technological innovation , from industrial machinery to AI , has repeatedly driven economic growth by improving productivity and expanding opportunity . <p> This recognition of innovation stands in contrast to the environmental consequences of that very progress . While technology has lifted economies , it has also contributed to rising emissions and resource depletion . <p> Faced with this tension , society often looks to history for reassurance . Past warnings of collapse have not always come true . Lord Kelvin underestimated the sun 's lifespan due to limited understanding of nuclear fusion . Thomas Malthus predicted food shortages that never materialised . <p> However , today 's climate warnings are different . The Planetary Boundaries Science Lab recently confirmed that seven of the nine boundaries essential for sustaining life have been breached , with ocean acidification added this year . These are not speculative forecasts but @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ expressions of concern remain muted . A " make everything great again " mindset has taken hold , driven by confidence in technology , especially AI , to solve or outpace global challenges . Financial markets are surging , regulators are retreating and companies are quietly abandoning climate commitments in favour of profit . <p> This shift raises a deeper question : have we returned to the days of unrestricted corporate capitalism , with the principles of King IV governance quietly set aside ? There is clearly a lot of uncertainty about the future path of the global economy . <p> The IMF has estimated that the cost of coping with climate change is far more severe than the cost of mitigating it . Given that climate change is here to stay , why have financial markets seemed to shrug it off ? <p> The most optimistic scenario is that the IMF and others are simply wrong . The AI boom could usher in a new era of innovation and productivity . In this view technology will resolve the challenges facing society , and allow progress without trade-offs . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ leaders such as Donald Trump and Xi Jinping , for policies that favour national champions to gain economic advantage . This approach contrasts with the view that the biggest contributors to climate change should bear the societal costs of their actions and that global regulations are necessary to manage global challenges . <p> A more sceptical perspective suggests that financial markets , with their characteristic short-term focus , have yet to fully reckon with the long-term consequences of global warming . Instead , their attention remains fixed on the present earnings cycle , which has exceeded expectations . <p> Recently , the Prudential Authority released its roadmap on climate risk for financial institutions . This guidance note expects financial institutions to carefully assess their resilience to climate risk . <p> At the same time , the US is clearly rolling back its climate commitments and allowing its companies to compete without considering the nonfinancial implications of their decisions . This makes it more difficult for other countries to compete with the US and follow through with their own climate commitments . <p> What does this all mean for investors and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to become despondent . However , despite sustained fossil fuel subsidies from vested interests , market incentives are leaning towards cleaner energy . <p> Despite worsening planetary boundaries , corporate earnings forecasts have risen , recession fears have eased and most central banks plan to keep lowering interest rates through 2026 . And , just as those three Nobel prize-winning economists showed , innovation often happens when one least expects it . <p> I began this article sceptical of anyone 's ability ( including my own ) to confidently forecast the future in the absence of perfect knowledge . To navigate an uncertain future , investors should remain agile , ready to seize the rewards of innovation while not ignoring the real and rising costs of climate inaction . Positioning portfolios for both possibility and risk is not just prudent ; it is essential . <p> Crosoer is Chief Investment Officer at PPS Investments . <p> Would you like to comment on this article ? Sign up ( it 's quick and free ) or sign in now .
